SAMUEL   STORY,   JR.,   LUPINE   PATH,  NEAR  SPRUCE  AV. 

"  O  Thou !  whose  word  from  solid  darkness  struck 
That  spark,  the  Sun ;  strike  wisdom  from  my  soul ; 
My  soul,  which  flies  to  thee,  her  trusi,  her  treasure. 
As  misers  to  their  gold,  while  others  rest : 
Through  this  opaque  of  Nature  and  of  Soul, 
This  double  night,  transmit  one  pitying  ray, 
To  lighten  and  to  cheer.    O  lead  my  mind 
(A  mmd  that  fain  would  wander  from  its  woe.) 
Lead  it  through  various  scenes  of  life  and  death. 
And  from  ea^  scene  the  noblest  truths  inspire.'' 


TOUCH  NOT  THE  FLOWERS. 

BY   MRS.    C.  W.  HUNT. 
"  O,  do  not  pluck  the  flowers ;  they  are  eacred  to  the  dead." 


Touch  not  the  flowersj  the  cherished  flowers, 

The  festal  gift  of  summer  hours; 

They're  holy  things ;  They  hloom  to  shed 

A  gladenin^  radiance  round  the  dead ; 

Their  glowing  cups  and  sweet  perfume 

Dissolve  the  shadows  of  the  tomb; 

'Twaa  no  vain  love,— the  love  that  gave 

Their  vernal  freshness  to  the  grave. 

The  snowy  marble's  sculptured  height, 

May  seem  to  thee  a  prouder  sight, 

And  ye  may  read  in  language  lair, 

High  names  and  deeds  emblazoned  there ; 

But  can  its  gorgeous  splendor  vie 

With  the  imperial  lily's  dye? 

Its  shrine  a  purer  record  be 

Of  all  that  bmds  the  lost  to  thee? 

Tonch  not  the  flowers ;  we  know  not  death 

Amid  iheir  loveliness .  each  wreath 

That  floats  apon  ihe  summer  gale 

Bears  saddening  tones  from  sorrow's  wail ; 

O !  can  ye  mark  their  bloom,  nor  feel 

The  truth  their  bursting  buds  reveal. 

That  earth  her  sacred  trust  must  yield. 

Whether  from  bower  or  tented  field? 

TherCj  v^here  yon  simple  daisy  rears 

lis  smiling  head,  w^lth  many  tears 

They  laid  a  fair  young  bride  lo  rest, 

Touch  .not  the  flower  her  love  hath  blest ; 


Within  its  clustering  petals  lie 
Memories  and  hopes  that  cannot  die ; 
Her  spirit  o'er  its  leaves  hath  shed 
A  life  that  animates  the  dead. 
How  vain  the  costly  pile  to  rear 
O'er  those  who  scorned  such  trappings  here; 
Swift  lime,  with  strong,  o'ermastering  power, 
Prostrates  high  tomb,  and  lowly  flower; 
But  summer's  breezes  shall  renew 
The  rose's  bloom,  the  violet's  hue; 
Not  so  the  carved  and  fretted  stone- 
It  springs  no  more  ;  its  glory's  gone. 
Touch  not  the  flowers ;  O,  can  there  be, 
Childhood,  a  holier  type  of  thee  ? 
A  filler  image  of  thy  doom  ^ 

Than  the  wild  floweret's  transient  bloom  ^ 
Lei  the  pure  sculpture  gleam  for  him 
Before  whose  breath  Ihe  world  grew  dim, 
But  spare  lo  purity  the  shrine 
Upspringing  by  a  band  divine. 
Touch  not  the  flowers ;  the  fervent  prayer, 
Poured  o'er  the  erring  slnmberer  there, 
On  incense  pinions  shall  arise, 
With  blissful  chasienings  to  the  skies. 
God  speaks  in  every  gloriood  hue, 
Bright  words  of  promise  unto  yoa  ; 
O'er  all  his  healing  love  he  sheda  : 
Xnuch  not  ihe  flowers.     They  are  the  dead's. 


FAREWELL  OF  THE  SOUL  TO  THE  BODY, 

BY   MRS.    L.    H.    SIGOURKEY. 


Companion  dear!  the  hour  draws  nigh. 
The  senleiice  speeds^io  rfte,  to  die. 
So  long  in  mystic  union  held, 
So  clo^e  with  strong  embrace  compell'd, 
How  canst  thou  bear  Ihe  dread  decree, 
Thai  strikes  thy  clasping  nerves  from  me  ? 
—To  Him  w^ho  on  this  mortal  shore, 
The  same  encircling  vestment  wore, 
To  Him  I  look,  to  Him  I  bend, 
To  Him  thy  shuddering  frame  commend. 
— If  J  have  ever  caus'd  thee  pain, 
The  throbbing  breast,  ihe  burning  brain. 
With  cares  and  vigils  turn'd  thee  pnJe. 
And  scorn'd  thee  when  thy  strength  did  fail- 
Forgive  !— Forgive  I— thy  task  doth  eease. 
Friend!  liover! — let  us  pan  in  peace — 
If  ihou  didst  sometimes  check  my  force, 
Or,  trifling;  stay  mine  upward  course, 
Or  lure  from  Heaven  my  wavering  trust, 
Or  bow  my  drooping-wmg  lo  dusl — 
I  blame  thee  not,  ihe  strife  is  done, 
I  knew  thou  wert  the  weaker  one, 
The  vase  of  earth,  the  trembling  clod, 
Constrained  to  hold  the  breath  of  God. 
—Well  hast  thou  in  my  service  wrought, 
Thy  brow  hath  mirror'd  forth  my  thought, 
To  wear  my  smile  thy  lip  hath  glow'd, 
Thy  lear,  to  speak  my  sorrows,  flowed, 
Thintj  ear  hath  borne  me  rich  supplies 
Of  sweetly  varied  melodies. 
Thy  hands  my  prompted  deeds  have  done, 


Thy  feet  upon  mine  errands  run — 
Yes,  thou  hast  mark'd  my  bidding  well, 
Faithful  and  true  !  farewell,  farewell. 
— Go  to  thy  rest.    A  quiet  bed 
Meek  mother  Earth  with  flowers  shall  spread, 
Where  I  no  more  thy  sleep  may  break 
W^iih  fever'd  dream,  nor  rudely  wake 
Thy  weaned  eye. 

Ofa,  quit  thy  hold. 
For  thou  art  faint,  and  chill,  and  cold, 
And  long  thy  gasp  and  groan  of  pain 
Have  bound  me  ptiyiug  in  thy  chain. 
Though  angels  urge  me  hence  to  soar, 
"  Where  I  shall  share  thine  ills  no  more, 
— Yet  we  shall  meet.    To  soothe  thy  pain 
Remember — we  shall  meet  again. 
Quell  w^ith  this  hope  the  victor's  sUng, 
And  keep  it  as  a  signet-ring, 
When  the  dire  worm  shall  pierce  thy  breast, 
And  nought  but  ashes  mark  thy  rest, 
W^hen  stars  shall  fall  and  skies  grow  dark, 
And  proud  suns  quench  their  glow-worm  spark, 
Keep  thou  that  hope,  to  light  ihy  gloom, 
Till  the  last  trumpet  rends  the  tomb. 
— Then  shall  thou  glorious  rise,  and  faifj 
Nor  spot,  nor  stain,  norw^rinkle  bear. 
And,  1  with  hovering  wing  elate, 
The  bursting  of  thy  bonds  shall  wait, 
And  breathe  the  welcome  of  the  sky— 
"No  more  to  part,  no  more  to  die, 
Co-heir  of  Immortality." 


*^Scots  wJia  hoe." 


Sons  of  Pilgrims,  from  afar, 
Scourged  by  Priests'  unholy  war, 
Denied  the  liglfi  of  Bethlehem's  star, 

Rejoice,  your  sons  are  free ! 
Free  to  praise  Jehovah's  throne, 
Proclaim  a  worship  of  their  own, 
Reap  the  fruits  their  sires  have  sown. 

Heavenward  Liberty  i 

Here  just  rulers  from  your  kin, 
Freemens'  voices  usher  in. 
Laws  from  them  have  ever  been 

Those  you  most  approved ! 


Here  our  youth  we'll  train  aright. 
Ungodly  power  to  curb  its  mighi, 
The  joys  of  life — an  equal  right, 

By  foreign  power  unmoved! 

Speed  the  boon  thus  nobly  planned, 

Their  growth  will  crown  and  bless  the  land, 

Worih  and  Science — hand  in  hand, 

Will  make  it  rich  in  siore  I 
Protect  the  Mothers'  sovereign  right, 
The  sons  will  bask  in  earth's  delight. 
And  virtue's  nerve  impait  ihem,  might  — 

To  joy  for  evermore. 


WOMAN. 

BT   XATHAXIEL   DEARBORN. 


Dame  Naiure  had  finished  her  toil  — 
'Twas  all  good  iii  the  world  to  her  eye.^  — 

But  she  dwelt  with  peculiar  delight 

On  her  last  and  best  work  —  'iwas  a  prize  - 

"  My  labor  was  arduous,"  she  said, 
"  With  materials  loo  rough  I  began, 

And  my  taste  was  not  good  as  yon  see, 
For  with  them  I  finished  a  man  ; 

But  woman,  I  moulded  you  la-:t  I 

With  a  delicate  hand  I  progressed  — 

Truth  and  innocence  placed  in  your  heart. 
And  with  beauty  and  love  did  the  rest  I 

To  man  I  must  e'en  trust  this  flower. 

With  this  charge,  if  they  ask  to  be  blest  — 

Protect,  love  and  honor  the  fair, 
Rememb'ring  its  high  heaven's  behest  ! ''

A   BALLAD. 

BY   XATHAMEL   DEARBORN. 


In  a  Stormy,  wint'ry,  midnight  hour, 

Seventy-six  years  ago. 
Seventeen  hundred  and  seventy-fourj 

Some  rorlsmouth  boys,  we  know, 
Scaled  the  great  lower — Newcastle's  fort, 

Chained  there  its  leader  foe, 
Took  sixteen  cannoii  from  their  port,— 

Guns  sixty,  thro'  the  snow  I 
Gunpowder,  too,  was  for  them  there, 

Full  barrels,  ninety-seven, 


All  trotted  off,  securely,  where 

They  could  not  them  replevin: 
No  blood  was  shed — no  wife's  dismay, 

Or  children  wrapt  in  w^oe, 
They  simply  seized  the  power  to  slay, 

Seventy-six  j'ears  ago. 
The  Granite  chaps  ^vitll  truth  relate, 

The  daring  of  its  hardy  scions, 
T'  achieve  and  elevate  their  State; 

Or  beard  unruly  Brilbh  Lions.

For  sel/j  dear  self—is  all  our  aim  I
